This plenary session includes the debate and adoption of the opinion on the proposal for an organic law amending the Criminal Code for the eradication of forced or non-consensual sterilization of persons with disabilities who are legally disabled. As an organic law, this initiative must be put to a final vote which, in accordance with Rule 131.2 of the Rules of Procedure, must be announced in advance by the Presidency. Therefore, pursuant to this provision, I announce that the votes will not take place before 18 o'clock. ACTS RELATING TO THE STATES OF ALARM, EXCEPTION AND SITE: - No, no, no, COMMUNICATION FROM THE GOVERNMENT FOLLOWING THE REAL Decree 900/2020 OF 9 OCTOBER, DECLARING THE STATE OF ALARM TO RESPOND TO SPECIAL RISK SITUATIONS FOR UNCONTROLLED TRANSMISSION OF INFECTIONS CAUSED BY SARS-COV-2. ParlaMint-ES_2020-10-15-CD201015.u2	On 9 October, the Government notified the Congress of Deputies of the adoption of Royal Decree 900/2020 of 9 October, declaring the state of alarm to respond to situations of special risk due to uncontrolled transmission of infections caused by SARS-COV-2. This royal decree, which was issued pursuant to article 116.2 of the Constitution, was transferred to the Constitutional Commission. For its part, pursuant to Rule 165 of the Rules of Procedure and for the purposes of the provisions of the Rules of Procedure, this item is submitted to the plenary session of the Congress. I give the floor, on behalf of the Government, to the Minister for Health. [[Applauds]]
ParlaMint-ES_2020-10-15-CD201015.u3	Madam President, ladies and gentlemen, good morning. I want my first words to be a reminder to all the people who have died from the COVID-19 and to convey all our affection and support to their families and people nearby. I would also like to convey a message of encouragement to all people affected by the disease right now and to those who are recovering from its effects. I wish them all a speedy recovery. I would also like to thank the exemplary behaviour and civic commitment that Spanish citizens continue to demonstrate, both in the implementation of health recommendations and in particular in the implementation of quarantines and isolations to protect their health and that of all. Finally, I would like to convey a message of appreciation and gratitude to all health professionals who continue to leave their skins working in very difficult circumstances with a full and committed commitment; a recognition that I extend to all those who, from their various activities and responsibilities, have worked and worked to address this pandemic. Ladies and gentlemen, I appear before the plenary of this House to report on the state of alarm statement adopted by the Council of Ministers meeting in extraordinary session on Friday, 9 October. A state of alarm that affects nine municipalities of the Autonomous Community of Madrid for a period of fifteen days, limiting mobility in these territories and giving legal coverage to the coordinated action in public health that the Interterritorial Council of the National Health System adopted on 30 September and that was not ratified in part by the Superior Court of Justice of Madrid for reasons of a formal nature. The state of alarm, as you know, was decreed by virtue of article 4 (b) of the Organic Law 4/1981 of 1 June of the States of Alarm, Exception and Site, which empowers the Government of Spain, in the exercise of the powers conferred on it by article 116.2 of the Constitution, to declare the state of alarm in all or part of the national territory when there are health crises that result in serious alterations of normalcy. Ladies and gentlemen, last June 21st ended the state of alarm started in March and extended successively by this House. It is therefore not the first time that a measure of this kind has been dealt with in Parliament. On 21 June the cumulative incidence in our country was 8.51 cases per 100,000 inhabitants. In parallel, since 9 June, the Government was addressing the later phase of that state of alarm with the promulgation of Royal Decree-Law 21/2020, which we know informally as a new standard and which this House is already treating as a bill. On 16 July, the Interterritorial Council of the National Health System approved the Early Response Plan in a pandemic control scenario by COVID-19. As you know, at this new stage the Ministry of Health has a relevant role in the field of coordination, but it is the autonomous communities that are competent to adopt containment and control measures. The epidemiological context tells us that different transmission scenarios coexist in different territories that may require different measures. In this context, the Ministry of Health has been working in collaboration with the Autonomous Communities with a daily monitoring of the evolution of the pandemic and capacities for response both at the bilateral level and throughout the system. Thus, we have proposed and approved the adoption of coordinated actions in public health. Since then, three packages of measures have already been adopted in this regard. The first of these coordinated action packages was unanimously agreed at the Inter-Territorial Council Plenary on 14 August. The second package of coordinated actions was approved, with a view to the start of the school year, on 27 August, following the joint meeting of the Inter-Territorial Council and the Sectoral Conference on Education. The third package of coordinated actions between the Ministry of Health and the Autonomous Communities was agreed on 9 September in relation to four areas: VOCID vaccination, screening, coordination with local authorities and launching new rounds of the National Seroprevalence Study. On 30 September, as I have already mentioned, new coordinated public health measures were adopted with measures for municipalities with more than 100 000 inhabitants. The epidemiological situation is very dynamic and changing and we must all be prepared to activate and disable the different scenarios in a very short period of time. Experience at the national and international levels in the pandemic suggests that the sooner and more forcefully action is taken, the more impact it will have on reducing the transmission of the virus, and the sooner it will make it possible to recover the previous situation. Ladies and gentlemen, it is important to stress that this second wave is fortunately very different from the first. However, mobility remains a very important contagion mechanism and sometimes it must be controlled, without, fortunately, reaching home confinement. Mobility restrictions have been a common transmission control tool that have employed territories of all sizes over the months. As you will remember, Catalonia was the first to use it in early summer in Lérida and in the region of El Segria; also in Galicia in July, in several localities of A Mariña, in Lugo. In Murcia, cities such as Lorca, Jumilla and Totana have also suffered mobility restrictions in July and August and until last week mobility restrictions have also been applied in these same localities. In addition, the two Castiles have applied mobility restrictions in several municipalities of different sizes, and are currently confined on a perimeter basis, including, for example, the city of León. The Valencian Community, Navarra, La Rioja, Aragón, Cantabria, Extremadura and Baleares have also had to take measures to restrict mobility in different municipalities and recently Andalusia, in the city of Linares, in Jaén. In short, ladies and gentlemen, the autonomous communities are making quick decisions and are anticipating to prevent further increases. Let me refer to two very recent cases: that of the Autonomous Community of Catalonia, which announced yesterday more restrictive measures for the next fifteen days, such as, for example, the closure of bars and restaurants, with the aim of reducing as much social interaction as possible in order to avoid even stricter measures in the future; and that of the Autonomous Community of Asturias, the Principality of Asturias, one of the autonomous communities where in recent months there has been one of the lowest incidences in the whole country, but which, given the increases it is experiencing in recent days, sees the need for restrictive measures to prevent major evils. The autonomous communities are therefore acting with determination and strength, anticipating themselves to prevent them. I would like to thank you from this rostrum, and I would also like to thank the work of the municipal authorities, whose collaboration is key in the fight against the pandemic. Ladies and gentlemen, let me then detail the chronology of events regarding the evolution of the pandemic in the Autonomous Community of Madrid over the last three weeks. On 17 September, the Vice-President of the Autonomous Community of Madrid, Don Ignacio Aguado, asked the Government of Spain for help because the situation in the Community of Madrid, he literally said - I open quotes - 'is not going well' - I close quotes. On the same day, the President of the Government sent a letter to the President of the Community of Madrid, Mrs Isabel Díaz Ayuso, requesting a meeting at which they could analyse the health situation and study together how to strengthen the means to overcome the critical moments as soon as possible, always for the benefit of the citizen. The meeting between the President of the Government and the President of the Autonomous Community of Madrid was held at the headquarters of the Regional Government, in the Puerta del Sol, four days later, in particular on Monday, 21 September. At this meeting, it was agreed to establish an area of strengthened bilateral cooperation between the two administrations and to establish a timetable with the aim of reducing the number of cases registered in this territory. That same Monday afternoon the COVID-19 Group was formed, of which we are part the Minister of Territorial Policy and Public Service and myself, as Minister of Health, as well as the Health Adviser and the Vice-President and Spokesman of the Government of the Community of Madrid. This group also has the advice and participation of the Centre for the Coordination of Alerts and Emergencies of the Ministry of Health and of officials and technicians of the Health Council of the Community of Madrid. They also support this group two structures - a territorial unit and a technical unit - that have been meeting. On Friday, the 25th, in view of the evolution of data in the Autonomous Community of Madrid, I myself publicly transferred the recommendations of the Ministry of Health, which consisted of adopting more extensive restrictions, stricter limitations than those which the Autonomous Community of Madrid intended to adopt. These recommendations were also moved to all the meetings we held the previous days. On Monday, the 28th, I had the opportunity to meet again with the health adviser of the Community of Madrid, as a meeting prior to the meeting of the COVID-19 Group held on Tuesday, the 29th of September. At this meeting, the regional Government accepted the criteria for confinement and the measures proposed by the Ministry of Health, as publicly conveyed in a statement, expressing satisfaction with this understanding. The Ministry of Health then proposed this package of measures for adoption at the plenary session of the Interterritorial Council of the National Health System the following day, 30 September. Ladies and gentlemen, as I said earlier, the Ministry of Health has been working with the Autonomous Communities on a daily basis to monitor the evolution of the pandemic and the capacities for response. In this regard, the role of the Inter-Territorial Council of the National Health System and its subordinate bodies as a governing body and guarantor of the fairness and cohesion of the system is essential. It is in this context that, since August, the ministry and the autonomous communities have agreed to take coordinated actions in the field of public health, as I have been able to detail before. The fourth and final package of coordinated public health actions was approved on Wednesday, 30 September, by a large majority, at the Interterritorial Council of the National Health System, with a very specific objective: to double the contagion curve in the territories most affected by the pandemic in order to achieve greater benefit in public health and to minimize the social and economic impact on the population. The agreed measures are minimum measures, which apply in municipalities with more than 100 000 inhabitants, with a cumulative incidence of 500 or more cases per 100 000 inhabitants in the last 14 days; secondly, a positive percentage in the results of diagnostic tests of active infection with COVID-19 greater than 10 %, and thirdly, that this municipality is part of an autonomous community with an occupancy of beds of intensive care units greater than 35 %. In those localities that meet these requirements, at least the eleven control and response measures and the two recommendations contained in the document approved by the Interterritorial Council of the National Health System must be implemented. These measures include, inter alia, restrictions on the entry and exit of municipalities, with exceptions on grounds of force majeure and others, such as visits for health, education or work reasons and for financial activities or for return to the municipality of residence, among others; limitation to six in the number of groups of persons who can meet; limitation of campgrounds in places of worship to one third, with a minimum distance of 1.5 metres; restrictions on wakes to fifteen people in the open air and ten people in closed spaces; the campgrounds in commercial areas limited to 50 %, with closure of the establishment at twenty-two hours; in the hotel shop, a campground limitation to 50 %, prohibition of bar consumption, the capacity of tables cannot exceed six people, customers cannot be admitted after twenty-two hours and the closing time cannot exceed twenty-three hours, and restrictions on campgrounds in sports facilities. In addition, the autonomous communities with municipalities with agreed indicators should strengthen their capacities for early detection and control of the disease, as well as in the area of primary care, as set out in the Early Response Plan in a scenario of control of the pandemic by COVID-19. This is what was agreed at the Interterritorial Council of the National Health System on 30 September. It was also recommended to avoid unnecessary movements or displacements in these municipalities and it was considered desirable for the autonomous communities to approve special plans in municipalities less than 100,000 inhabitants with a high cumulative incidence of new cases. However, as you know, at the meeting of the plenary of the Interterritorial Council of the National Health System held on Wednesday, 30 September, the Autonomous Community of Madrid voted against the adoption of this agreed measure which it had accepted the previous day. As a collegial decision of the Inter-Territorial Council, this fourth declaration of coordinated actions is binding and sets out a number of minimum criteria. It entered into force on Friday 2 October at night. As I have pointed out above, the vast majority of autonomous communities have been making decisions in recent months along the same lines to control transmission, almost in all cases with lower indicators than those agreed two weeks ago. Returning to the chronological exposition, in the case of the Madrid Community the regional government published the order on Friday 2 October implementing the measures agreed at the Interterritorial Council. Finally, on Thursday, October 8, he learned of the decision of the Madrid High Court of Justice not to ratify the order of the autonomous community regarding the measure that limits fundamental rights, that is, the measure that refers to the perimeter confinement of the municipalities affected by the agreed epidemiological criteria. I would like to stress that the reasons given by the court are not of substance, but of mere legal authorization to adopt them. Ladies and gentlemen, on Thursday 8th after returning from his trip to Algeria, the President of the Government of Spain called on the President of the Community of Madrid to address the existing legal alternatives and to be able to maintain the measures provided for in the order partially revoked by the High Court of Justice of Madrid. In that conversation, the president proposed three scenarios to the president of the Autonomous Community of Madrid. First, that the Community of Madrid, as a competent authority in the field of health, should issue an order pursuant to Article 3 of Organic Law 3/1986 of 14 April on special measures in the field of public health, something which it had not previously done when publishing the order agreed upon in the interterritorial and which was partially revoked by the High Court of Justice of Madrid. The second scenario was that the Government of the Community of Madrid should request the declaration of state of alarm so that the Government of Spain, together with the Community of Madrid, could declare and ratify the measures in question and thus give them all legal cover. And, third, that the Spanish Government should declare the state of alarm, after approval by the Council of Ministers, without the need for the President of the Madrid Community to make a request in advance. The Government of the Community of Madrid requested more time on the same Friday morning and then decided to continue implementing measures which, in the light of the data, were no longer sufficient to protect the health of the citizens of Madrid, but of the rest of Spain. Ladies and gentlemen, from the end of the state of alarm until today 1415 people have died from COVID-19 in the Community in Madrid. Last Friday there were 3361 people hospitalized and 498 people in intensive care units in the Autonomous Community of Madrid. The obligation of the Government, of any Government, of any Administration is to curb the virus, doubling the transmission curve and protecting the health of citizens, those living in the same territory and those living in neighbouring territories. In view of this situation, it was very important that Madrid's high level of infection did not extend to the rest of Spain. The measures we adopted at the Interterritorial Council of the National Health System on 30 September were clearly in this direction: to protect the affected populations as much as possible, to preserve the assistance capacity and to minimize the risk of exporting a serious epidemiological situation from one territory to another, ultimately reducing transmission rates below the levels of risk established by international agencies. The Madrid High Court of Justice had stated that the legal formula adopted was insufficient to limit fundamental rights such as those of movement, on the grounds of not substantive but formal reasons. Therefore, given the evolution of epidemiological data and taking into account that the Community of Madrid was the only community with municipalities affected by the declaration of coordinated actions of 30 September that had not been ratified judicially the measure concerning the restriction of movements, the Government decided to adopt the state of alarm in this autonomous community to give legal coverage to the decisions taken within the Interterritorial Council of the National Health System. The measures are the same as those I referred to earlier, since the decree adopted last Friday by the Council of Ministers specifically regulates the restriction of entry and exit of the affected municipalities, which are nine; namely: Alcobendas, Alcorcón, Fuenlabrada, Getafe, Leganés, Madrid, Móstoles and Parla. At the time of approval by the Council of Ministers, all of them met the three requirements set out above. Ladies and gentlemen, I would like to stress that the aim of the Government has always been to protect the health of the entire population. Health protection was also our priority on Friday, 9 October, at the beginning of a bridge, and the urgent objective was to give legal coverage to the measures taken and to prevent the uncontrolled spread of the virus. Let me just offer you the mobility data in the Community of Madrid during this last bridge of 12 October compared to the bridge of 1 November 2019; this comparison is more representative since it is a bridge that lasted also three days, while on 12 October last year it fell on Saturday. The data compared, therefore, with last year's data tell us that, according to the Directorate-General for Traffic, there has been a decrease in the number of exit movements of vehicles in Madrid by 46%; in rail transport, the data record a decrease in the number of passengers by 79%; in air transport, the data provided by AENA show a decrease of 84% in the number of exit and entry passengers at Adolfo Suárez Madrid-Barajas airport. The situation, ladies and gentlemen, is worrying, very unstable, very fragile and we cannot lower our guard, because if there is something that has taught us this virus is the enormous capacity to spread and spread among the population; I insist, the situation is worrying, very worrying. As you know, the duration of the state of alarm is fifteen days, which in addition to the previous days on which the order of the Community of Madrid was in force total twenty-three days with restrictive measures, we trust that sufficient to stabilize the situation. It is necessary to monitor how the pandemic evolves in these municipalities and work hard to bend the curve. Ladies and gentlemen, the government's aim is to get out of the emergency, not just the state of alarm. According to yesterday's data, the national average of the cumulative incidence at fourteen days in our country is in 265 cases per 100,000 inhabitants, but we know that the averages show significant territorial differences, so that the cumulative incidences between 82 cases per 100,000 inhabitants of the Autonomous Community of the Canary Islands or 101 cases per 100,000 inhabitants of the Valencian Community vary considerably, to much higher incidences such as in Navarre, Melilla or Madrid, more than double the national average. In no case, I repeat, under no circumstances can we standardize such high figures of cumulative incidence, which continue to indicate a high-risk situation that should be the exception and not the norm. To give us an idea of the size of the situation in which we find ourselves, our national average quadruples the threshold considered high risk by the ECDC, by the European Centre for Disease Prevention and Control, which considers worrying incidences higher than 60 cases per 100,000 inhabitants in fourteen days or a positivity in PCR tests higher than 3%. Moreover, the European Centre for Disease Prevention and Control establishes two cut-off points above this incidence of 60 cases per 100 000 inhabitants, which considers them at high risk: 120 cases per 100 000 inhabitants and 240 cases per 100 000 inhabitants. That is to say, over 120 cases per 100 000 inhabitants ECDC ranks the areas in a very high risk situation and from 240 cases per 100 000 inhabitants places them at an extreme level of maximum risk. The real perspective of the situation in Spain is that not only the national average is at that maximum level, but also that some territories are in a completely exceptional situation of doubling that maximum alert level. Therefore, the perspective that we must all have, I insist, is to lower the cumulative incidence not by 500 positive cases per 100 000 inhabitants, but by far more, around or below 100 positive cases, because that is the only way we can talk about doubling the curve. It is true that the incidence of COVID-19 in one territory is not the only indicator to assess population risk and that proposals for action should also consider other criteria for transmission or early detection capacity and for the care system, but it is also true that the cumulative incidence, measured in cases per 100,000 inhabitants, is a priority indicator, recognized and recommended by international bodies to assess the effectiveness of the control measures implemented. Therefore, although this second wave is, fortunately, different from the first one throughout Europe, so far, we cannot standardise incidence figures that are far above the areas considered safe in Europe. Ladies and gentlemen, that is what is happening in the countries of our environment. Europe is currently experiencing significant increases in daily cases and cumulative incidences in several countries and is taking drastic measures to control the pandemic. Paris and other French cities, such as Marseille, Lyon, Toulouse and Montpelier, have been on high alert since 5 October after having exceeded the 200 cases per 100,000 inhabitants and had 100 cases among the elderly and more than 30% of the beds of occupied critics. The 15-day maximum alert state includes restrictive measures, such as the total closure of cafés and bars and the ban on student parties, and the French Government has just announced that it will return from this Saturday to the state of health emergency, from which it left in July, and will impose a curfew in Paris and other major cities between 9:00 a.m. and 6:00 a.m. in the next four weeks. The United Kingdom also has a context marked by restrictions in the various territories since the closure of schools and bars in Northern Ireland to restrictions on travel in Wales. The Italian Council of Ministers also adopted restrictive measures two days ago, on 13 October, with a cumulative incidence of less than 100 cases at national level. The measures, which will last one month, include hourly limits of bars and restaurants, closure of discos and dance halls, suspension of parties both outdoors and in closed spaces and the recommendation not to celebrate parties or relate to more than six non-living people. In Germany, cities such as Berlin, Frankfurt or Cologne, which have exceeded the threshold of 50 cases per 100,000 inhabitants, have taken measures such as limiting private meetings to the hours of bars and restaurants, among others, and the German Government has already announced that it intends to extend the restrictions and reduce the threshold to 35 cases per 100,000 inhabitants in the light of the clear increase in contagion in Germany. And Belgium, the Netherlands and the Czech Republic have also announced important measures in the face of increased cases. Belgium closed bars and cafes and banned meetings of more than four people for one month from 9 October. The Netherlands will carry out a month of partial confinement of its citizens, with cafes and restaurants closed for four weeks, since yesterday. In the Czech Republic they have also announced closures of schools, bars and restaurants for several weeks. Finally, Portugal, which so far had a favourable situation, also declared yesterday the state of calamity, applying measures and new restrictions in the face of the increase in cases recorded in recent days. Ladies and gentlemen, prevention and control measures at Community level are only part of the management of the pandemic. As the Early Response Plan that I mentioned earlier shows, managing includes several essential aspects, which I would like to recall again because they are still very much in force in this second wave. First, the plan highlights actions to ensure the proper functioning of public health services dedicated to responding to COVID-19 and to ensure the implementation of monitoring protocols. This undoubtedly requires ensuring the adequate capacity of human resources dedicated to the field of public health. To reinforce this point, the Government has supported the management of the autonomous communities with two thousand military trackers and a digital tool to support the identification and follow-up of contacts, as well as the Radar VOCID-19 application. Secondly, the plan contains another essential aspect of management, which is to ensure the proper functioning of early warning and early response systems. We know that in the areas of greatest incidence it is more complicated to keep surveillance systems in time and form, but it is crucial to spare no effort to avoid delays in reporting, in order to allow us to assess the situation in real time. Thirdly, the Early Response Plan also includes a number of specific measures from contingency plans in primary and hospital care, including sufficient human resources to respond to extra activity associated with increased transmission by COVID-19. We have to bear in mind that a single pathology, such as that produced by COVID-19, is occupying almost 10% of hospital bed resources and almost 20% of ICU resources, on a national average, with some territories doubling these occupations. Fourthly, the Early Response Plan contains another essential element for the effective management of the pandemic, which I would like to highlight, in this second wave, that it is the availability of places for isolation of cases and quarantine to ensure that these measures are implemented efficiently in situations where they cannot be properly carried out in homes or places of residence. These are living documents - the Early Reform Plan - that we are updating. In this regard, guidance is being worked out from within the Public Health Commission to implement it in accordance with the new epidemiological reality. Ladies and gentlemen, I have said this on numerous occasions, and I will not tire of repeating it. Public health is the only thing that matters to us, like the vast majority of citizens, so we must act in a health code, act before, preventively and forcefully, just as the countries of our environment do. We are faced with a public health problem and we must continue to take those measures that are necessary to prevent contagion and reduce transmission and thereby save lives; measures to protect the health and safety of citizens and to further strengthen the National Health System; measures, ultimately, to control the pandemic, to double the curve again and then keep it down. Our first objective, ladies and gentlemen, is to double the curve of the virus again, and I am sure that everyone here, all political groups, share it. We must deal with the virus with individual and collective responsibility, with strength and with all the strength and resources of our public health system. We have to keep up the effort we are making as a society, because there is still a long time of coexistence with the virus, a long stage in which we will all have to continue to comply with the health recommendations that we know work: interpersonal distance, hand hygiene and mask use; a long stage in which we will all be forced to adapt to the new circumstances, both at school and in cultural activities, and also in the way we do business or how we relate to our family and friends. Therefore, ladies and gentlemen, let us not lower our guard, keep our alert and exercise our prudence. I conclude by insisting on something I have repeated over all these months, which is the need for us to work together and with the greatest possible unity. The common adversary to all of us is the virus and unity is what makes us strong in front of it. It is not time for provocations or divisions; it is not time to distract us in sterile disputes or discussions, but to continue to hoist our shoulders together and have all the necessary means to deal with the virus. If we are to remain effective against the virus, we must continue to strengthen coordination and cooperation instruments in the field of public health. That is what citizenship demands and what we owe to those who are facing the disease every day in health centres, emergency services, hospital plants and ICUs across the country. Thank you very much, ladies and gentlemen. [[Applauds]]

And when it comes to discussing the next day, on 30 September, it turns out that Madrid is unravelling and voting that it is not. I'm just describing the facts, I'm not saying anything else. What happens next - and I think it is important to point it out for the consequences that may come later in the future -? The Law on Cohesion and Quality of the National Health System of 2003 hangs instead of the Organic Law on Special Health Measures of 1986, which had been used by all the autonomous communities that had previously needed to confine populations, and important populations in some cases. And all these orders of the autonomous communities based on the 1986 Act, the Organic Law on Special Measures in Health Matters, have been ratified by the courts of justice. Therefore, there was a basis and there is a basis to do so. In this case it has not been so because it has been based on the Law of Cohesion and Quality of the National Health System and the party that is at the head of the Autonomous Community of Madrid it is the one that resorts to its own norm. There is a situation that, in fact, could have been avoided more than likely. This is how we understand it, acting from responsibility and, as an extraordinary exercise - although we think and have been demonstrated and ratified by the courts of justice in some communities and others that current health legislation is sufficient -, if necessary under some circumstances, the autonomous community itself could pull out of that instrument, but retain its powers. That using as an excuse the artificial conflict - the artificial conflict - provoked in Madrid they intend to re-centralize competences is something unjustified and, of course, we will never support it. This is our position. We have the necessary legal means, if we act responsibly in the exercise of our own competences, and the basis has to be collaboration and transparency, not using the pandemic for the general policy debate - it is unacceptable that this be done - and humility in the face of a situation of unpredictability in the evolution of the pandemic and in the situations to which we can all be seized. ParlaMint-ES_2020-10-15-CD201015.u63	Thank you very much, ladies and gentlemen, for your interventions. I will, with your permission, make a final reflection. In Europe we are in a second wave, with Community transmission in most European countries, also in Spain. We are in a context of uncertainty, there are still many things we do not know, but we do know that this second wave is not the same, fortunately, as the first one, because we detect more cases, because the profile of the cases is of younger people, because the lethality so far is much lower and because we have also taken measures that help us prevent this second wave. We also know that the way to combat it is with early detection, with many PCRs, with many tests, as our country is doing, at the head of Europe in this area, and with isolation and surveillance of those who have given positive results. And we also know from the accumulated experience of July, August and September, as well as from what we see our European partners do, that we must act quickly, without delay, and with a contunence proportional to the reality and epidemiological situation of each territory; if I may, act preventively even, anticipating us when we see that the accumulated incidences begin to grow. This is what the autonomous communities in our country and our European partners in Europe are doing. The concern caused by the situation in the Autonomous Community of Madrid, which is very worrying, the concern it generates does not come from the legal instrument that we have used to give legal coverage to a decision of the Interterritorial Council of the National Health System; the concern comes - please, let us focus on this - from the epidemiological situation, from a very high cumulative incidence, from a high percentage of hospital bed occupancy, around 20%, a little below these last few days, and from an occupation of the ICUs - having increased, and that is to say, I recognise it - about 40%, with a level of positivity of the CRPs of around 20%. This is what is causing concern, not the legal instrument we have used to cover a measure that was already very much needed, by far. I ask caution when evaluating the data, and not rush. There are delays in notification not due to negligence of any kind, logical when there are so many cases; there are delays in notification that must be evaluated with caution, calmly and listening to those who know how to interpret it properly, which are experts and technicians, perhaps also caused by the impulse to antigen tests that some autonomous communities are giving. Therefore, prudence and not precipitation, prudence and not precipitation in assessing the data. And I ask you not to worry, we will assess the Government's actions and you will have the opportunity, often already, to say whether we have done it right, wrong or worse or better. We will assess, if you think so, together calmly what we have done well, what needs to be improved and how our country needs to be better prepared for future pandemics. We will decide this together and we will, but now I ask you to focus on fighting this second wave, and I ask you heartily and earnestly. I do not expect any reward from anything or anyone, I hope that our country, with all of us together, can win as well, as it did with the first, this second wave. That is where I can commit and ask - I also believe that I am in the position, since I commit it - institutional loyalty, coordination between administrations and respect for the framework that we have in our country, which is that of a composite State which, in my opinion - I may be wrong, there may be conflicting views - is functioning reasonably well. I think if we do that, we'll do a lot better. And there they will allow me to say once again that the pulse that the government has is not with anyone, it is not with any Administration, it is not with any mayor, it is not with any city and it is not with any territory, it is a pulse only against the virus, and I ask that we all join this pulse against the virus. By the way, without leaving anyone behind. I've been asked before that I don't forget about those people who need transparent masks, because they have a disability, and we don't forget about them. We are working to ensure that those available on the market can be approved. So, ladies and gentlemen, all together, please, we beat this virus. Thank you very much. ParlaMint-ES_2020-10-15-CD201015.u64	Thank you very much, Minister.
ParlaMint-ES_2020-10-15-CD201015.u65	We begin with the point corresponding to the validation or repeal of royal decrees-laws. First, there is the debate on the validation or repeal of the Royal Decree-Law 28/2020 of 22 September on distance work. To present the Royal Decree-Law, the Minister of Labour and Social Economy has the floor on behalf of the Government.
ParlaMint-ES_2020-10-15-CD201015.u66	Good afternoon. I compare before you today to expose the content and scope of Royal Decree-Law 28/2020, which regulates distance work. This rule provides for a form of work which, without being new, has acquired a relevance and a specific weight unknown so far in our country. In order to strengthen its advantages, to guarantee the working conditions of the workers and to ensure that all this consolidates better labour and business dynamics, the present royal decree-law has just been lit up, a rule, as you know, which is the result of social dialogue, debate and pooling, and for which I call today for support in its parliamentary ratification. They will agree with me that the agreement within the framework of the social dialogue raises a question for all of us, representatives of the citizenry, especially in these days, and it directly calls us, it terrifies us, I would say. The question is the one that follows. Is this not the way in which the institutions act, primarily political parties and politicians, in particular? Because I assure you that contradictory interests and different positions between employers and workers in the initial positions of the government were, but, yes, the social actors, also the government, knew to preserve on all things a fundamental issue: the general interest in the interests of the party. Let me tell you that I honor you, that you are quite an example, and as a minister of the Government and as a government we feel legitimately proud. The question is: Is there any political conclusion that can be drawn from such a fact? Frankly, I think so, because this country, its citizenship, is not mistaken, it is in need of dialogue and abhors the rift. Yes, ladies and gentlemen, there is still a gap in this House and much dialogue is lacking. With the rift, we run the risk of the institutions being permanently resented. With dialogue, legitimate interests find a single channel, the democratic one, the one to which we owe ourselves. So let us follow that path. Believe me, it's a citizen cry. In this historic moment of the country in which we play so much, dialogue is not a way, it is the only way. With regard to dialogue, with this royal decree-law we are faced with a rule that will undoubtedly mark the future of an essential part of the labor relations of the present century, of the twenty-first century, and will be a binding reference of the legislator in the coming times, by the level of theoretical and practical contributions of the Social Dialogue Bureau that, as I have told you, have been reflected in this royal decree-law. We feel legitimate pride in the country. Spain is today a reference between the countries of our environment in this area, and we have been shown this by our European partners, in particular, for their explicit interest. Thirdly, the social dialogue supports the decree that we have ratified today and that we are defending today. All the effort, all the transactions, all the hours employed have received their fair compensation. And fourthly, this royal decree inaugurates a series of legislative advances in the coming months that will place the paradigm of labor relations at the height of Spain of the twenty-first century, and we will do so as we have done this royal decree-law that I present to you, always with social dialogue, and I have the hope and confidence that it will open the way. That is why I wish, in Parliament, the seat of national sovereignty, to solemnise the gratitude of the Government and my own to the social partners, trade unions and employers' organizations, which have contributed so much to making this possible. The Royal Decree-Law on Distance Work is a rule that meets a peremptory need at this time. The development of information technologies has facilitated alternative forms of work to strictly face-to-face ones. This has been reflected in a clear trend towards increased distance work in recent years, particularly in the form of telework. This boom explains the numerous rules, agreements and documents that have dealt with distance work: ILO Convention 177 on Homework was one of the first, in the already distant year 96; the European Framework Agreement on Telework of 2002, reformulated in 2009, which was an agreement, as you know, of great importance. This agreement has inspired the Spanish rule and also a large part of the legislation that will recently be adopted by countries in our environment. The health crisis has accelerated the evolution of distance work. It is estimated that during the pandemic the incidence of remote work has increased from 5 % to 34%, proving to be the most effective mechanism to ensure the maintenance of activity and to ensure prevention against contagion. Its use, moreover, has allowed to warn and explore in a short space of time its possibilities, but also its risks. The possibilities that open up work at a distance are obvious. Remote work allows a better organization of one's own time, which has an undoubted personal dimension and also a dimension that results in one's own company, promoting a higher quality of work and increased productivity. The regulation that we are submitting to this House today also has a dimension that transcends the strictly productive or labour-intensive one. On the one hand, remote work is related to the green economy, because it reduces displacement and seeks greater sustainability and efficiency in energy consumption; and, on the other, it can promote the repopulation of rural areas and promote territorial cohesion, allowing the place of residence not to be determined exclusively by the location of our companies' headquarters. This is an important incentive to improve the quality of life at local level, whether rural or urban. In any case, it is a step in the line of the demographic challenge facing our country. However, remote work also carries risks, such as those arising from isolation with its consequent consequences in the field of psychosocial risks or even specific forms of violence, such as cyberbullying. In addition, isolation - and this is not a minor issue - can limit trade union action. In turn, the use of computer tools poses significant challenges in the field of prevention. It can trigger technological stress and requires special attention to the specific risks associated with sedentary lifestyle or the intensive use of screens. The company is also facing new challenges in the field of organisation, such as the possible shortcomings in the exchange of information between workers or the need to establish specific control mechanisms. However, the main risk of remote work is the insecurity that has so far led to the lack of adequate regulation. The PP's labour reform, which in this area was limited to article 13 of the Workers' Statute, did not serve either to establish guarantees or to provide security or to enable distance work to deploy its full potential for both sides; a frustrating change, as was the case everywhere with that reform. This royal decree-law emerges to cover this important gap and constitutes, for this purpose, a systematic and comprehensive regulatory framework that establishes a complete floor of guarantees, while at the same time entrusting the capacity for adjustment and adaptation to the social partners. One of the main features of the regulation of distance work contained in this Royal Decree-Law 28/2020 is that it establishes the pillars for the development of healthy forms of distance work. He is aware of the potentialities and problems that arise from complete in-personity, full-distance work, and therefore promotes mixed forms of work organization, in which in-personity and distance alternate and enhance with total naturality. This mixed dimension is reflected in the definition of distance work itself, which is that carried out on a regular basis in the home of the worker or in the place freely chosen by the worker. The actual decree-law specifies the scope of this regular nature, which comes - as you know - when the 30% percentage of distance work is exceeded, thus avoiding the day-to-day effect of some companies and productive sectors. This percentage incorporates a factor of legal certainty and is consistent with the dynamics of alternation between remote and face-to-face work promoted by the Royal Decree-Law. This Regulation also ensures the proper use of distance work through the establishment of limits on its use, both by minors and by persons employed in training. In the case of minors, the presence of specific risks from distance work, such as those arising from physical and mental fatigue, isolation or lack of safety, advise the establishment of limitations, as accepted and required by the Spanish and European Union legislation on the prevention of occupational risks. This is the case for contracts in practice and for training and learning which by their very nature require some face-to-face contact in order to ensure that training or professional practice is effective under adequate and sufficient supervision by the undertaking. For this reason, the actual decree-law on distance work requires that, in the case of contracts with minors or contracts on training or apprenticeship practices, the minimum percentage of invisibility is 50 %. However, the key feature of the regulation of distance work is its voluntary nature, as outlined in the 2002 European Framework Agreement. Only in the framework of voluntarity is distance work configured, and only in it can it be modified with the inescapable collaboration of collective bargaining. In this context of full voluntarism, the distance working agreement is of fundamental importance, because it faithfully contemplates the will of the parties and provides the necessary security in the event of possible conflicts. Therefore, the text of the Royal Decree-Law sets out its regulation in detail. One of the main challenges facing distance work is that of working and rest times. The very nature of this work makes the boundaries between professional and personal life sometimes blurred, which requires that a norm such as this one devotes special attention to it. The main budget of this rule is respect for the right to personal and vital time of people, not only because it is the main purpose of the labour law since it exists, but also because it is required of us by our Constitution, international law and European Union law. The characteristics of distance work, combined with the use of information technologies, require that the standard pay particular attention to the right to disconnection. Therefore, this royal decree-law not only recognizes and applies to distance work the right to disconnection, which is a right linked to prerogatives as decisive as health or privacy, but also provides the necessary framework for this disconnection to be effective. It establishes, therefore, the obligation that the specific schedule should be set out in the distance work agreement, setting up a flexible working time system around that schedule for the benefit of the worker. Flexibility in the management of time by the worker opens up many other possibilities of interest, such as improving training. For this reason, the Royal Decree-Law also amends article 23 of the Workers' Statute, which, as they know, regulates workers' training rights and provides that persons who are regularly studying are entitled to access to work at a distance. This forecast is consistent with the fundamental role that continuous training must play in our labour relations system as part of an effective employment policy that will at all times facilitate the provision of the tools necessary for workers to improve their situation in the labour market. The hourly flexibility that distance work provides also has a dimension related to the reconciliation of responsibilities that the Royal Decree-Law undertakes with the necessary guarantees to avoid the perpetuation of roles of care in women. Remote work is not a special form of work designed to reconcile, and from this budget the philosophy of the royal decree is precisely part of it: it is not a form of conciliation. The pandemic has taught us that work cannot be simultaneous in place and time to care, neither when working in the office nor when working at home. This regulation of distance work seeks to permanently banish the image of women or men trying to care for their children, while attending the screen of their computer. Strengthening this principle of separation and establishing in a clear way that distance work does not neutralize the rights of conciliation is another fundamental basis of this royal decree. Thus, a person working remotely will have the same right to adapt his working day, as a person who works face-to-face. Avoiding the perpetuation of roles of care in women through distance work is one of the transversal threads of this royal decree. In this sense, collective bargaining, which is intended for a wide range of competences throughout the text, should promote co-responsibility and prevent the perpetuation of gender stereotypes. The principle of gender mainstreaming is also applied throughout the framework, as required by the Organic Law on Effective Equality of 2007, incorporating distance work as a tool for the protection of victims of gender-based violence. One aspect of particular relevance in the field of distance work is that of costs and expenditure. This rule establishes the guarantee mechanisms necessary to ensure that the situation of persons working at a distance is equal to that of face-to-face workers, so that it is the undertaking which must adopt and maintain the necessary means for the activity, as well as to compensate for costs related to work or care in the event of technical difficulties. The natural areas for the specific determination of these costs and expenses which this standard recognises and promotes are undoubtedly collective bargaining and the distance working agreement. Both perform the function of ensuring that the worker is adequately compensated and at the same time provide security to the business organisation itself. In this drafting there has been a careful task of translating and applying the fundamental rights and also the ordinary rights of workers to the peculiarities of distance work. This ensures equal treatment at all levels with face-to-face workers, including communication with representatives, the exercise of collective rights, remuneration, promotion or training. In addition, there are strong guarantees relating to the exercise of fundamental rights in the company, related to the right to privacy or data protection. Remote work requires control mechanisms that are essentially different from those used in face-to-face work, but in any case they must respect the right to privacy. It was also necessary to incorporate into the text the test of suitability, necessity and proportionality, as you know, required by the Constitutional Court itself, in order to consider that a particular control system, whether digital or not, is in conformity with the constitutional text. All this has been transferred to the royal decree-law in relation to work distance in a way consistent with Spanish legislation and jurisprudence, as well as with European Union legislation. In addition, the Royal Decree-Law provides for intense protection in the field of occupational risk prevention, adapted to the particularities of distance work, which requires special attention in the evaluation and preventive activity and its particularities, for example in the field of psychosocial risks. Therefore, the possibility of access to the place of provision of services for the purpose of carrying out the corresponding risk assessment, which will be conditional, as they know, in any case, on the express authorization of the worker. The scope of effectiveness has been given special attention in this rule, which has resulted in the corresponding amendments to the sanctioning regulations, the Law on Offences and Penalties in the Social Order, and in the procedural regulations themselves, the Law on the Regulation of the Social Jurisdiction. Specific infringements and sanctions have been established in this regard in cases where the obligation to formalize the distance work agreement is not adequately met. A specific, rapid and preferential judicial procedure has also been developed to ensure that complaints relating to the right of access, reversal and modification of distance work are resolved as quickly as possible. Another of the fundamental features that characterize the Royal Decree of Work at Distance is that it places in a central place the collective bargaining itself. Social dialogue is indeed the backbone of this royal decree-law, the content of which is the result of the agreement of the social partners, a negotiating dimension that reinforces the legitimacy of the rules and also ensures their effectiveness. This negotiated nature is therefore a fundamental element to be highlighted and especially valued. The strong and coordinated exit from the health and economic crisis caused by the pandemic must also underpin the foundations of the new stability challenges facing companies and workers in the immediate future. This dimension intertwined in collective bargaining is transferred to the whole articulated of the royal decree, with express calls to the collective agreement in such relevant aspects as the establishment of preferences or the very definition of distance work, which can be stipulated in collective agreement by means of percentages different from those fixed in the norm. In this way, the present standard of distance work incorporates negotiation into the normal and ordinary dynamics of distance work with a completely different perspective from that which it has governed in labour standards over the past few years. The rules on distance work regulated in this royal decree finally set out a series of transitional rules which set out a detailed framework of specific forecasts in view of the circumstances of the performance of the work. Therefore, different treatments are envisaged, depending on whether or not an agreement or prior agreement was in place according to which distance work was being carried out, and the situation of distance work resulting from the pandemic is expressly envisaged. In short, the Royal Decree-Law on Distance Work is a global, integral and cross-cutting rule that guarantees fundamental rights and transfers to the field of distance work the labour rights, both individual and collective, of face-to-face work. It is a detailed and comprehensive rule that provides legal certainty and establishes the appropriate mechanisms for its full effectiveness. [[The lady]] In addition to the rule governing distance work, there are more content in this royal decreeley. First, the Royal Decree-Law provides for the extension of the Me Care Plan, which will extend until 31 January 2021. The Plan Me Care, as you know, allows the adaptation of days and schedules with a flexibility hitherto unknown, facilitating reduction of days of even one hundred percent of them. Secondly, a number of necessary measures are set out in response to the circumstances in which the pandemic is evolving in relation to the health sector and also to the railway sector. The entry into force of certain administrative rules on electronic records and archives is also postponed by the Royal Decree-Law. As you know, it is imperative that reference be made to the concurrence both in the regulations on distance work and in the additional and final provisions of this article of the extraordinary and urgent need required by article 86 of our constitutional text for the approval by the Government of a royal decree law. Firstly, this regulation addresses the current situation of the health crisis and the need to provide certainty. From the beginning of the pandemic, remote work has been a tool that has served both to maintain business and work activity and to prevent contagion. It is a mode of organization that adapts to the current health situation and that must be properly regulated, taking into account the fulfilment of its purpose during the crisis, but also the evidence of its boom as a new work model. Secondly, from the perspective of business organisation, distance work is a formula that can enable a more rational and efficient organisation of working time. At a time when companies are facing processes of recovery, renewal or re-adaptation of great importance for the future, it is the responsibility of the public authorities to make available to them all the instruments that can help to consolidate business projects on a stable basis in a scenario in need of certainty such as the current one. Thirdly, the solution proposed by this royal decree is based on a shared diagnosis with the social partners, aimed at giving immediate satisfaction to the needs of businesses and women workers, which also the social dialogue has understood to be an impossible task. The text we propose to validate today is an opportunity for companies to have the necessary tools aimed at consolidating their projects and a decisive improvement also in the working and living conditions of citizens. That is what companies and workers have agreed with this Government at the social dialogue table and that is what is being discussed today in this royal decree-law, but, above all, what I ask you to validate today is the commitment of all public institutions, including the legislative branch, with companies and with workers at a time when they need the rules to offer solutions that are appropriate to reality. This reality certainly includes the most vulnerable, and this royal decree also expresses the firm will of this Government to bring about a new social right that was born as one of the most supportive measures in history for all people and families who are in need and who now more than ever need to be protected by public policies. I'm done. The pandemic has revealed to us the importance of remote work. The adoption of this rule opens the door to the labour legislation of the twenty-first century, a new time in our country's labour relations. We will do so, as always, with the commitment to decent, stable and quality employment that is present in all the measures we take and with the awareness that any new time in labor relations or in the modes of organization will necessarily come from the hand of rights and decent working conditions. Thank you so much. [[Applauds]]

In other words, the three dimensions of the digital divide converge in a marked way in the emptied Spain: there is a lower level of income and training, there is a more ageing population and there are very important gaps in the broadband network. We have been saying many times that emptied Spain is also to a large extent disconnected Spain, and we must put an end to this disconnection; a disconnection caused by the lack of coverage and broadband, which, as I said, has to be resolved urgently. If large companies or the free market are not able to provide this broadband extension throughout the territory, the State, on its own initiative or through public undertakings, must act. We are faced with a case similar to when our entire territory was endowed with drinking water or electricity, since it is a universal good recognized as such by the United Nations and must be extended well with the Market Law, or with direct action by the State. Teleworking for us must be an opportunity for demographic rebalancing, for territorial cohesion and for reducing the problems of depopulation. We can't let an opportunity like this forget us again, we can't let this train pass. Because each year that passes without the total coverage of the territory will be many of the opportunities lost for personal decisions of residence or location, we demand that before or parallel to this law the total coverage of the digitization of the optical fiber in Spain is developed. Thank you very much.

Of these indefinitions this royal decree suffers, indefinitions that have risks, as it has everything that is not regulated and that opens doors to the widening of the gender gap, that of inequality or that of exploitation even. There are many issues that remain in the air by assuming that the situation is the same for all, and it is not, not all collectives have the same access and the same possibilities, not all houses are prepared for remote work, and the digital divide is actually a socio-economic gap because it is the lower socio-economic classes that do not have the same access to the tools. There is another central aspect, among the issues that are not clear in the decree, which refers to conciliation. You, Minister, have spoken about the subject and have been clearer than this decree. In the same way that it happened with the closure of schools, when the weight of care fell more on women - this has happened, it has been so - we believe that we need to fine-tune more so that women do not become entangled again between conciliation and work. In this eternal and continuous confinement in the home between work and care, telework can become an insurmountable trap for women. In this regard, there is a clarification point that provides recent data on the scientific activity of men and women during the pandemic: men have produced and sent to specialized journals far more scientific articles than women. This can be extrapolated to any field of work. This lack of reconciliation resulting from teleworking can have these consequences, and future consequences for the professional and personal development of women. Telework, if no measures are taken with regard to conciliation, can reposition us under a new glass roof. These were the issues I wanted to comment on and, finally, one more that perhaps comes out a bit from this decree, goes beyond it, but I think it is important to point it out. As in times of great changes, opportunities should be taken advantage of, it would not be wrong to look a little further and focus the future on something that is already being set up in other European countries as a good option. I am talking about going the way from teleworking to the concept of flexible work. A step forward to rethink the future and propose a change to a flexible conception of work, allowing a real balance between professional and personal life and which could materialise in the weekly day of thirty-five hours in five days or thirty-two hours in a four-day working week, as proposed by the Prime Minister of New Zealand. This, moreover, has a twofold positive side, as it would reactivate the country's internal tourism and boost the economy. I want to say with this that the possibilities for everything that needs to be done are more open now than ever. A final fact or comment on social dialogue, Minister, of course that social dialogue is positive and necessary, but I think we should not forget that if social dialogue becomes a right to veto progress in rights it is not so positive. We will support the two decrees with our vote because it is urgent and necessary to regulate telework, despite these loopholes that I have commented on. Eskerrik asko.
ParlaMint-ES_2020-10-15-CD201015.u73	Thank you very much. [[Pause.-A cleaning service worker proceeds to disinfect the speaker's rostrum]] For the Basque Parliamentary Group, Mr Barandiaran Benito has the floor. [[EAJ-PNV]]
ParlaMint-ES_2020-10-15-CD201015.u74	Well, I don't know if you're almost ready, Madam President. Ladies and gentlemen. This royal decree-law, presented to us today by the Government or approved by the Government and already incorporated into the law, incorporates into positive law what is nothing more than the elaboration of the principles of the European Framework Agreement on Telework signed on 16 July 2002, which was subsequently updated in 2009. To this end, it is necessary to develop article 13 of the Workers'Statute, which deals precisely with remote work, and incorporates in this mode of provision the regulation of the basic aspects of teleworking as a reality in full swing and development, based on the exclusive or predominant use of computer, telematic and telecommunications means and systems. It is also understood by such telework that the regular mode is provided for at least 30 % of the day during a reference period of three months or the equivalent percentage according to the duration of the employment contract, and also limits its application with respect to those concluded with minors and contracts in training and apprenticeship practices. It is based on the elaboration of certain principles which are then laid down in the positive law which refer to equal treatment and opportunities and non-discrimination, to the voluntarity and reversibility - fundamental, we understand - to the obligation of the written form, to the right to vocational training and promotion, to the provision and maintenance of means and equipment, to the payment and compensation of expenses - to which I shall refer - which will be determined by agreement; the right to flexible hours and registration; the right to the prevention and evaluation of occupational risks; the right to privacy and data protection; the right to digital disconnection as a fundamental substantive element, in addition to other rights, and the right to the exercise of collective rights. As I said, they are rights and obligations already recognized in labour law itself, and it makes an explicit reference to the Data Protection Act with regard to the right to digital disconnection; that is a substantive element that we believe is properly developed. As a technical criticism, we see that this text is drafted in place of article 13 even though it could have been drafted in amendment of article 13, that is, that the Workers' Statute itself could have served as a support for the elaboration of this element of positive law, without prejudice to the fact that it maintains as it is in the field not of collective bargaining, but of the social agreement, it seems that it saves this technical inconvenience of the regulatory aspect precisely by the legitimation that it is granted not only by the formal elements, but also by the substantive elements to which it refers, and gives it an indisputable solidity. However, and also as a critic, we can say that the use of the mechanism of the royal decree-law, precisely when it is based on the social agreement, reveals a certain contradiction, of which we simply want to warn, but without putting any emphasis on it. Having said that, the existence of a material sphere sufficient for teleworking has been maintained as a whole; we also believe that there are essentially two elements: the right to disconnection, which underpins legitimation, and then also the reference to collective bargaining, so that those regulatory aspects that the text itself is going to leave open can be realized, and in that sense we believe that the reference to collective bargaining on which we are not going to make any kind of question is a positive element. In this sense, therefore, we have a positive assessment of the actual decreeley and we will not focus on more precise elements regarding its content, since the minister has been prolific, as always, in its development and we simply announce that we are going to vote in favour. Thank you very much.

From now on, working people will be able to voluntarily agree with the business side on the provision of distance work, either at home or in any other place that meets the conditions for it. That agreement shall be recorded in writing, taking into account the following points. We're done getting e-mails or wasaps at 10:00 p.m. If they pay us four hours we work four hours, if they pay us eight hours we work eight hours, but not twenty-four hours a day. They shall also have the right to training, professional promotion and the prevention of occupational risks. Working remotely can't mean playing physics, it can't mean you can't develop your career or you can't have more responsibility in the company. They will also, of course, be entitled to collective representation. Working remotely can't mean you're out of union representation. That would be a flagrant breach of the Constitution, which was the case in many cases. And, of course - and not least of all - they will have the right to stability in the workplace. In no case, I repeat, in no case, the refusal to work remotely will justify the termination of the employment relationship. This is important to reschedule. There are voices criticizing this new law saying that it does not effectively shield the law and that voluntariness implies being at the mercy of the employer on duty. ParlaMint-ES_2020-10-15-CD201015.u86	Thank you, President. Good afternoon, ladies and gentlemen. At this very moment there are millions of Spaniards in their homes and not precisely because they are teleworking, but because the inaction of this government has condemned them to an ERTE which, by the way, many of them do not even charge or, in the worst case scenario, have been engaged in the closure of their businesses. In the midst of this hurricane of mass destruction of businesses and jobs, telework has been the lifesaver of our economy. Strengthening the use of distance work in an orderly, flexible and legally secure way is important for Spain, especially at the moment. However, this royal decree has been more concerned to limit and hold hands with entrepreneurs than to really push away work as an opportunity for our companies and for workers from all over the world. You have spoken of solutions that contemplate the reality of this country, and that is what I would have liked you to have included in this royal decree, the business reality of our country. Because the reality of Spain is that almost all companies, almost 95% of the business fabric, are built by small and medium-sized enterprises, by SMEs with less than ten employees, and, on the basis of this reality, it would be useful to analyse each point of this royal decree and really understand how these measures will affect small and medium-sized enterprises in our country. Let us illustrate this reality with a simple example. Let us say that a Spanish entrepreneur from a common SME has suffered a major deterioration, if not the complete detention of his circulating asset, due to the pandemic and has seen in the telework an opportunity to digitize his business, to continue his activity with less fixed associated costs and to avoid dismissals caused by the limitations imposed by this government. This entrepreneur appreciates the option of distance work because he sees it as an opportunity to enable his business and not destroy employment. Well, do we manage with this royal decree to offer the businessman certainty as to what telework is going to cost him? No, ladies and gentlemen, we did not, because this royal decree says that the employer must cover the costs of distance work, but it does not even specify what specific costs the small and medium-sized businessman must pay, which, as you will understand, creates enormous uncertainty when planning his costs. Can we by this royal decree offer the worker certainty about the tax burden attached to telework? We do not, ladies and gentlemen, either, because this royal decree does not offer certainty about the increase in the IRPF that workers will have to pay as a concept of remuneration in species that, as you know, is subject to the arbitrariness of the criterion of Finance. It would have been desirable, Madam Minister, for you to set these items as remuneration-free tax and contribution-free, but you have not done so either. Can we ensure with this royal decree that the entrepreneur can freely manage his resources and assets freely, so that he can make the best decisions for the maintenance of his activity and employment? No, we do not, either, because this royal decree, in its article 5.1, eliminates the employer's ability to manage its factors of production, so that it cannot even implement telework through article 41 of the Workers' Statute, which, as you know, minister, covers all kinds of changes in working conditions when there are proven economic, technical, organisational or production reasons. However, the employer cannot apply it for distance work, i.e. that this royal decree further compromises the viability of a company that requires changing working conditions to, for example, maintain the level of employment or avoid bankruptcy. Can we implement, as has been the case so far, the implementing priority of the company agreement with this royal decree? No, ladies and gentlemen, of course not.
